## What the assignment grades

- **Three hard gates:** GitHub link, a public URL on a free tier, and the full chat log. Missing any one means no evaluation, so I'd deploy an empty skeleton on day 1.
- **Depth over breadth:** "a few features done extensively beat many fitting minimal requirements", and you must justify your scope. The README's scope section is effectively a deliverable.
- **Explainability:** you must be able to explain the code, so I'd use plain, conventional Laravel with no extra layers.

## Ambiguities and how I'd read them

| Part | Ambiguity | My reading |
|---|---|---|
| Store | What is a "product"? | A sellable variant, as on Eneba: title × platform × region ("Elden Ring Steam Key GLOBAL"). |
| Homepage | "Each block gets content from its own backend service or database" | Not microservices. A `homepage_blocks` table sets which blocks appear and in what order; each block type has its own resolver class and data source. |
| Search | How typo-tolerant? | Postgres `pg_trgm`, one implementation behind both the header search and the store's name search. |
| Product page | Are sellers real users? | No. Sellers are seeded data, with no seller login or dashboard. |
| Checkout | Guest cart? | Yes. The PDF's flow diagram puts sign-in only at checkout, so the cart lives in the browser. |
| Auth | How much? | Register, login, logout. No password reset, email verification, or social login. |
| Styling | "Follow the design reference" | No reference is attached, so the live eneba.com is the reference. |
| Data | Where do products come from? | Unspecified. We seed roughly 150 products. |

## Architecture

- **Repo:** one monorepo with `api/` (Laravel, REST), `web/` (Vite, React, TypeScript, React Router, TanStack Query, Tailwind), and `docs/ai-log/`.
- **Hosting:** Vercel for the frontend, Render (Docker) for the API, Neon for Postgres.
- **Auth:** Laravel Sanctum bearer tokens.

| Decision | Simpler choice I'd take | What we give up |
|---|---|---|
| API style | REST | GraphQL fits homepage blocks well but adds a library to learn and explain. |
| Search | `pg_trgm` in Postgres | Meilisearch ranks better, but needs a second always-on service and a sync job. |
| Payment | Simulated gateway behind an interface | Stripe test mode is more realistic but needs webhooks and key reservation with expiry, about a day of work. |
| Cart | Browser storage, re-priced by the server | No cross-device cart; avoids guest-cart merging on login. |
| Auth transport | Bearer token | Cookies are safer against XSS, but cross-domain cookie setup is a classic time sink. |
| Rendering | Client-side SPA | No server rendering or SEO. |
| Frontend host | Separate from the API | Two deploys, but the page shell loads instantly while Render's free instance wakes up. |

From memory, Render's free Postgres expires after 30 days and its free web services sleep when idle. That is why I suggest Neon; I'll verify current terms before we commit.

## Data model

An offer is one seller's price for one product, and a key is one unit of stock belonging to an offer.

```
platforms        id, name, slug
regions          id, name, slug
products         id, slug, name, description, cover_image_url, platform_id, region_id,
                 type, release_date, sales_count          (trigram index on name)
sellers          id, name, slug, rating, sales_count
offers           id, product_id, seller_id, price_cents, original_price_cents, is_active
                 unique (product_id, seller_id)
license_keys     id, offer_id, code, status (available|sold), order_item_id, sold_at
users            id, name, email, password
orders           id, user_id, status (pending|paid|failed), total_cents,
                 payment_reference, idempotency_key, paid_at
order_items      id, order_id, offer_id, product_id, product_name, seller_name,
                 unit_price_cents, quantity
homepage_blocks  id, type, title, position, config (json), is_active
banners          id, title, subtitle, image_url, link_url, position, is_active
```

- **Stock is not a column.** It is the count of available keys, so it cannot drift from reality.
- **Cheapest price is computed at query time**, not stored on the product. At this data size that is fast and always correct; storing it is the path at scale.
- **Money** is integer cents in a single currency (EUR).
- **Order items snapshot** the product name, seller name, and price, so history survives later price changes.
- **Best sellers** sort by `sales_count`, which is seeded and then incremented by real purchases.
- **Deals** are offers where `original_price_cents` exceeds `price_cents`.

## API

```
GET  /api/homepage                 ordered blocks, each with type, title, data
GET  /api/products                 q, platform[], region[], min_price, max_price, sort, page
GET  /api/products/{slug}          details + all offers, cheapest first
GET  /api/search/suggest?q=        top 6 for the header dropdown
GET  /api/filters                  platforms, regions, price range
POST /api/auth/register | login | logout      GET /api/auth/me
POST /api/cart/quote               re-prices a browser cart, flags sold-out lines
POST /api/orders                   auth; pays and returns the order with keys
GET  /api/orders, /api/orders/{id} auth; owner only
```

Store filters live in the URL, so results are shareable and the back button works.

## The three areas you flagged

- **Offers:** the product page lists every seller's offer with price, rating, and stock. The cart holds offers, not products, because the buyer chose a seller.
- **Search:** results rank by prefix or substring match first, then trigram similarity, then popularity. Queries under three characters fall back to prefix matching, where trigrams are weak.
- **Checkout:** `POST /api/orders` runs in one database transaction:
  1. Re-price from current offers; if the total changed, return 409 with a fresh quote.
  2. Lock the needed keys with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`; if there are too few, return 409.
  3. Charge the fake gateway; on decline, return 402 and sell nothing.
  4. Mark keys sold and the order paid.

  An `Idempotency-Key` header makes a double-click return the same order. Because payment is synchronous, no reservation timers are needed.

## Priorities

- **Deep, with backend tests:** offer model and product page, search, checkout and key delivery.
- **Solid but standard:** store filters, sorting and pagination, auth, homepage with three or four block types, order history.
- **Styling:** shared design tokens taken from eneba.com, with the closest match on the header, product card, and product page.
- **Cut, and listed in the README:** seller accounts, admin UI, reviews, wishlist, multi-currency, emails, refunds, server rendering, genres.

Two backend tests failed on the first run and were fixed:
- **Search threshold:** PostgreSQL's default trigram threshold misses a single dropped letter ("mincraft" scores 0.58 against a 0.6 cutoff). The test now uses 0.4; the search increment needs to set its own threshold.
- **Test mistake:** one test set the price after the discount was calculated. That was my error in the test, not in the schema.

## What was created

- **API:** Laravel 13 on PHP 8.4 with Sanctum, a health route, 11 models, 4 enums, and factories.
- **Data model:** four migration files covering catalog, offers, orders/payments/keys, and homepage.
- **Frontend:** Vite, React, TypeScript, React Router, TanStack Query, and Tailwind, with a small API client and a placeholder page.
- **Environment:** `docker-compose.yml` with PostgreSQL 17 (dev and test databases) and a `Makefile` (`make setup`, `make dev`, `make test`).
- **Docs:** [README.md](README.md), [docs/ARCHITECTURE.md](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md), [docs/DEPLOYMENT.md](docs/DEPLOYMENT.md), and [CLAUDE.md](CLAUDE.md).

## Changes from the approved model

- **Checkout is three steps:** reserve keys, charge, then finalise. Keys gained a `reserved` state with an expiry, so no database locks are held during payment.
- **`payments` table added:** it keeps "money moved" separate from "buyer owns keys", so a crash between the two is recoverable.
- **Constraints in the database:** duplicate checkouts, double charges, and inconsistent key states are rejected by the database itself.
- **Concurrency proof:** a test with two real connections shows simultaneous buyers never get the same key. The full checkout tests come with that increment.

## Decisions made

- **Auth:** Sanctum session cookies, with the API proxied under the frontend's address.
  - Both `vercel.app` and `onrender.com` are public suffixes, so a direct cross-domain cookie would be blocked by Safari and Chrome Incognito.
  - One thing is unproven: that the login cookie passes through Vercel's rewrite. I'd test it on the first deployment; the fallback is serving the frontend from Laravel.
- **Hosting (verified today, nothing set up):** Vercel, Render, and Neon.
  - Render's free Postgres still expires after 30 days, which is why the database goes to Neon.
  - Render's API sleeps after 15 minutes and takes about a minute to wake.
- **Images:** no third-party cover art. Covers will be original images generated from the seed data, so rights are clean. Product names are still real game titles; say if you'd rather use invented ones.
- **Homepage:** one service class per block type, no microservices.

## Issues encountered

- **Homebrew is blocked.** This macOS account is not an administrator and Homebrew belongs to another account, so PHP and Composer run in Docker instead.
- **Docker Desktop started your other projects' containers** when I launched it. I left them alone; our database uses port 5434 to avoid a clash.
- **Laravel's skeleton shipped agent instruction files** telling AI tools to run a curl-to-bash PHP installer and install Laravel Boost. I did not follow them and removed the files.
- **Could not confirm:** whether Render asks for a card at sign-up.

## Readiness checks

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Backend tests | 53 passed |
| Frontend tests, lint, type check | 14 passed, clean |
| Production stack run locally, auth end to end (`make prod-check`) | 32 of 32 checks passed |

| Area you asked about | State |
|---|---|
| CORS | Only the frontend's address is allowed, never with credentials. |
| Sanctum session | Only requests from the frontend's address get a session, driven by one variable, `FRONTEND_URL`. |
| Session cookie | HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax, no Domain, destroyed server-side on sign-out. |
| CSRF | Forged cross-site requests are rejected with 419. |
| Trusted proxies | Enabled only in production; Laravel correctly sees HTTPS and the public host behind a proxy. |
| Database SSL | `verify-full`: from the production image, an untrusted certificate and a non-TLS server were both refused. |
| Secrets | No env files, keys, database hosts, raw transcripts or personal email are tracked, and the Docker image excludes `.env`. |

## Blockers and open points

- **Vercel cookie forwarding is undocumented.** Vercel's docs do not say how a rewrite treats `Set-Cookie`, `Cookie`, `Origin` or `Referer`. My local proxy stand-in proves our side only.
- **If it fails, diagnosis comes first.** A diagnostics route, off by default, reports what the server actually received, and [docs/DEPLOYMENT.md](docs/DEPLOYMENT.md) maps each failing check to a cause.
- **The successful `verify-full` connection to Neon is untested.** It needs Neon's real certificate; migrations run at start-up, so a failure will show in Render's log.
- **Render may ask for a card.** Its docs do not say. If it does, stop.
- **Known limitation:** a client calling the Render address directly can forge its IP. Sign-in is also rate limited per email, which that does not bypass. The full fix is a secret header from Vercel, which I'd add after the plain rewrite is proven.
- **Still your call:** raw transcripts stay unpublished by default.

## What I checked and changed

**`APP_URL` and `FRONTEND_URL`:** both must be the Vercel address, not Render's.
- `FRONTEND_URL` decides which origin may use the login session and CORS; the browser is on the Vercel address, so that is what its headers say.
- `APP_URL` is the root of links the API generates; visitors only ever see the Vercel address.
- The container now refuses to start if either is missing, not https, or has a path.

**Probing the production image directly found three real problems, all fixed:**
- **Broken redirect:** a URL with a trailing slash was redirected to `http://<api-host>:10000/…`, an unreachable internal address. The API no longer redirects at all.
- **Crash for signed-out callers:** a protected route asked for with a browser's `Accept: text/html` returned 500, because Laravel tried to redirect to a login page that doesn't exist. It now returns 401.
- **Forgeable host and client IP:** with all proxy headers trusted, a direct caller could set both. Now only "the visitor used https" is believed, and links are built from `APP_URL`.

**Direct API access:**
- **Sessions:** a visitor's cookie is bound to the Vercel host and is never sent to Render.
- **CSRF:** still enforced on direct calls.
- **Rate limiting:** sign-in and sign-up are limited by claimed visitor address, by edge address, and by email, with no shared secret needed.
- **One assumption is unproven until live:** that Render's edge overwrites the `CF-Connecting-IP` header. `make verify` sends a forged value and fails if the server accepts it; the fallback is Vercel's secret-header approach.

**Console error:** `/api/auth/me` now answers `{"data": null}` for a visitor instead of 401. A real failure (server or database down) still returns an error and the page shows "API is not reachable" rather than a sign-in form; both cases are tested.

## What I found

I called that address with read-only requests and compared the answers with what our code returns:

| Request | Our code | That server |
|---|---|---|
| `/api/health` | `{"status":"ok"}` | `{"status":"ok","database":"ok","pg_trgm":"1.6","time":…}` |
| `/api/auth/me` as a visitor | 200 `{"data":null}` | 401 `Unauthenticated` |
| `/api/sanctum/csrf-cookie` | 204 | 404 |
| `/api/_diagnostics` | 200 (switched on in `render.yaml`) | 404 |
| Web server | Apache | nginx |

No commit in our history ever produced those responses, GitHub `main` contains only my pushes, and your account has no other repository with "eneba" in its name.

There are two possible explanations:
- **Most likely, it is someone else's server.** Render service names are first come, first served across all customers, and the server calls itself "Eneba Marketplace", so it may be another candidate's project. In that case Render gave your service a different address, usually the name plus a suffix.
- **Or it is your service, built from something other than this repository.**

Either way, forwarding our `/api` there would have sent sign-up emails and passwords to a server we can't vouch for, and sign-in would not have worked.

## What I changed

- **`web/vercel.json` now fails safely.** `/api` is forwarded to a placeholder address that cannot exist, so a frontend deployed too early gets errors instead of talking to an unverified server. Routing is otherwise unchanged.
- **A backend must prove it is ours.** `make verify-backend API_URL=…` compares the server's answers and its reported commit with this repository. The full verification runs this first and stops before creating an account if it fails. Against the address above it stops, as expected.
- **Rewrite and fallback rules are checked.** A new script confirms every `/api/…` path is forwarded with its path intact, every app page gets `index.html`, the two rules cannot overlap, and nothing in the file alters cookies or headers.

How Vercel itself passes cookies, CSRF tokens and headers is still only provable on the live site, as before.

## Catalog phase: scope

- **In:** seed data, the store listing (filters, sorting, pagination), the product page with seller offers.
- **Out:** name search with typo tolerance, cart and checkout, homepage blocks, the Eneba styling pass.

## Data flow

```
Curated JSON in the repo ── seeders (same result every run) ──> PostgreSQL

Store page   /store?platform=steam&sort=price_asc&page=2
  URL is the single source of truth ──> GET /api/products?… ──> validated request
  ──> one query: products + cheapest in-stock offer ──> resource ──> typed response ──> cards

Filters      GET /api/filters ──> platforms, regions, types, price range (nothing hardcoded)

Product page /product/:slug ──> GET /api/products/{slug} ──> product + every active offer
  with its seller, cheapest first ──> details and an offers table to compare
```

Rules the API applies:
- **Price:** a product's listed price is its cheapest active offer that has a key available.
- **Money:** integer cents plus currency; the frontend only formats.
- **Stock:** a yes/no per offer; key codes never leave the checkout.
- **Validation:** unknown filters, sort values or out-of-range pages get a 422 with a message.
- **Typed responses:** Laravel resource classes define each shape, and TypeScript types mirror them.

## Acceptance criteria

**Backend, as integration tests on PostgreSQL**
- Listing returns the cheapest in-stock price per product, never a pricier or sold-out offer's.
- Each filter (platform, region, type, price range) narrows correctly, alone and combined.
- Each sort order is correct and stable across pages.
- Pagination reports page, page size and total; page size is capped.
- Invalid input returns 422; an unknown product returns a JSON 404.
- The product endpoint lists all active offers with seller details, cheapest first.
- No response contains a key code.
- The listing uses a fixed number of queries regardless of page size.
- Seeding twice produces identical data.

**Frontend**
- Filters, sort and page live in the URL; reload, share and the back button all work.
- Loading, empty and error states exist on both pages.
- Filter options come from the API.
- Type check passes against the shared response examples.

**Live**
- Seeded catalog is visible on the public site.
- `make verify` still passes, with a catalog check added.

## Decisions for you

1. **Product names:** real game titles with our own generated covers (my recommendation), or invented titles? This has been open since the foundation.
2. **Name search:** leave it to the search phase (recommended), or add a plain substring filter now?
3. **Sold-out products:** show them flagged and sorted last (recommended), or hide them?
4. **Keeping types honest:** shared JSON examples that backend tests must reproduce and the frontend type-checks against (recommended, no new dependency), or runtime validation with a schema library?

I'd build it in three reviewable steps: seed data and covers, then the API with tests, then the two pages and deployment.

## Games versus sellable variants

I split them, as you preferred.

| Table | Holds |
|---|---|
| `games` (new) | title, description, cover, type, release date, stored once |
| `products` | one variant: `game_id`, platform, region, name, sales count |

- **Removed from `products`:** description, cover, type and release date.
- **New rule:** a game is sold at most once per platform and region, enforced by a unique constraint.
- **One deliberate duplicate:** a product keeps its `name` ("Elden Ring Steam Key GLOBAL"). It is built from the three parts in one function, and the trigram search index needs a single column.
- **New migration, not an edit:** the original migrations already ran in production. It refuses to run if products exist; production has none.
- **Side benefit:** the product endpoint lists the same game's other platforms and regions.

## Seed data

- **Size:** 34 titles (26 games, 2 DLCs, 3 gift cards, 3 subscriptions) sold as 71 variants, with 272 offers from 8 invented sellers and 1,450 keys.
- **Deterministic:** every price, seller choice and key is computed from a hash, not random. Seeding an empty database twice gives an identical catalog.
- **Safe on every deploy:** re-seeding never revives a sold key or resets a sales count.
- **Covers:** 34 original generated images, one per game, shared across variants.
- **Sold-out examples:** three variants are marked sold out in the data.
- **Not fact-checked:** titles, release dates and platform availability come from my own knowledge, not a source I checked today. Please spot-check [games.json](api/database/data/games.json).

## Endpoints

`GET /api/products?platform=ea-app&sort=price_asc` (one item shown):

```json
{
  "data": [{
    "slug": "it-takes-two-ea-app-key-global",
    "name": "It Takes Two EA App Key GLOBAL",
    "type": "game",
    "cover_image_url": "/covers/it-takes-two.svg",
    "release_date": "2021-03-26",
    "platform": { "slug": "ea-app", "name": "EA App" },
    "region": { "slug": "global", "name": "Global" },
    "in_stock": true,
    "price": { "amount_cents": 1329, "currency": "EUR", "original_amount_cents": 1749, "discount_percent": 24 },
    "offers_count": 5
  }],
  "meta": { "current_page": 1, "per_page": 24, "total": 3, "last_page": 1 }
}
```

- **`GET /api/products/{slug}`** returns the same fields plus `game` (slug, title, description), `offers` (id, seller with rating and sales count, price, `in_stock`) and `variants`.
- **`GET /api/filters`** returns platforms, regions and types with product counts, the current price range, sort orders, and page sizes.
- **Pricing rule:** the listed price is the cheapest active offer that still has an available key.
- **Sold out:** `in_stock` is false and `price` is null. Such products sort after available ones in every order and never match a price filter.
- **Validation:** unknown filters or sorts, an inverted price range, or an oversized page get a 422 with a message per field. An empty value means no filter.
- **Change from my outline:** a page past the end returns an empty list, not a 422.

Full examples are in [web/src/contracts](web/src/contracts); a backend test fails if the API stops returning exactly those.

## Query performance

Measured on a laptop, on the seeded catalog and on a scratch dataset 170 times larger:

| Request | 71 products | 12,000 products |
|---|---|---|
| List, default sort | 5 ms | 117 ms |
| List with platform, region and price range | 5 ms | 25 ms |
| List, page 400 of 500 | n/a | 335 ms |
| Filters | 2 ms | 52 ms |
| Product page | 5 ms | 4 ms |

- **Query count is fixed:** five for the list, ten for the product page, whatever the page size or number of offers.
- **Indexes:** the plan uses the two built for this, cheapest offer per product and available keys.
- **Known limit:** at the larger size the unfiltered list computes every product's best price twice, once to count and once for the page. Storing the best price on the product is the fix if the catalog ever grows that far; I did not add it.

| Secrets audit | pass |

The new tests cover:
- **Pricing:** cheaper sold-out, inactive, reserved-only and sold-only offers are ignored, and the discount belongs to the cheapest offer.
- **Listing:** sold-out ordering in all four sorts, filters alone and combined, inclusive price bounds, stable pagination, and validation.
- **Safety and data:** key codes never appear, and the seeder is deterministic and idempotent.

## Ranking approach

One query (`Product::scopeSearch`) serves both the header suggestions (`GET /api/search/suggestions?q=`) and the store (`GET /api/products?q=`). The suggestions are therefore always the first six results the store would show, and a test asserts that.

**What matches.** The text is cleaned once (trimmed, lowercased, at most 80 characters and 6 words). Every word must match the product name, in any order:

| Word length | Matches when |
|---|---|
| 1 or 2 letters | it starts a word in the name |
| 3 or more | it appears anywhere in the name, or is a near miss of a word in it |

A near miss is `pg_trgm` word similarity of at least 0.5. I measured this on the catalog: the default 0.6 misses ordinary slips ("mincraft" scores 0.58), and 0.4 starts matching unrelated titles.

**Order of results:**

1. Tier: exact name or game title, then name starts with the term, then every word present, then typo matches.
2. Similarity score within the tier.
3. Purchasable before sold out.
4. Popularity (sales count).

Choosing another sort order (price, popularity, newest) re-sorts the same matches.

**Cost controls:**
- Suggestions take 5 queries and the store takes 6, whatever the input.
- Fewer than 2 characters is answered without touching the database.
- Suggestions are rate limited to 120 a minute per visitor.
- The trigram index is used for typo searches: 1 to 15 ms at 12,000 products.
- Rate-limit counters moved from the database cache to a file store, because counting each suggestion was costing 12 extra queries.

## Browser findings (live site)

- **Suggestions:** typing "eldn ring" made one request (about 0.6 s) and listed six Elden Ring products plus "See all results".
- **Keyboard:** arrow keys move the highlight while focus stays in the box; Enter on a suggestion opens the product and clears the box; Escape closes the list and keeps the text.
- **Store:** "witchr" + Enter opens `/store?q=witchr` with two Witcher 3 results sorted by "Best match".
- **URL as state:** ticking Steam gives `?q=witchr&platform=steam`, Back restores the previous results, and a shared link with search, region and sort restores all three.
- **Empty state:** "zzzzqq" shows "No products match" with a link to all products.
- **Phone width (375 px):** the search box takes its own row, the suggestions fit the screen, and there is no sideways scroll.
- **One issue found and fixed:** Render's firewall answers 403 for attack-like text such as `1 OR 1=1` before our code sees it. The store was showing "the server may be starting up" with a retry button; it now says "This search could not be run".

## Limitations

- Swapped letters in short words ("zleda") and a letter missing from a very short word ("rng") are not matched.
- Abbreviations and nicknames ("gta", "rdr2", "fifa") find nothing; only product names are searched, not descriptions.
- Accents are not folded; no title in the catalog has one today.
- On a page whose address contains firewall-refused text, the filter panel also fails to load, because the firewall reads the page address sent with each request. I left this alone rather than change request headers on the sign-in path.
- A one-letter search matching most of a 12,000-product catalog took about 110 ms in the database; the 71-product catalog is nowhere near that.

These are recorded in `README.md` and `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`.

One commit with the latest conversation log is saved locally and not pushed. Each live verification run leaves a throwaway `deploy-check-…@example.com` account in production.

Before implementing, review the existing order, payment, reservation, and license-key models against the current schema. I want to make sure the transaction boundaries and state transitions are correct before we build on top of them.

For the cart, keep the selected seller offer as the item identity, not just the product. Persist the cart locally for guests, then revalidate everything against the backend before checkout. Price changes, unavailable offers, insufficient stock, and duplicate items need to be handled properly.

For checkout, use the simulated payment approach we agreed on. Keep payment processing separate from inventory allocation, and make sure reservations expire safely if payment isn't completed.

Pay particular attention to concurrency and idempotency. Two buyers shouldn't receive the same key, repeated checkout requests shouldn't create duplicate orders, and a failed payment shouldn't permanently consume inventory.

I also want the order and payment states to be explicit. Think through what happens if the process crashes between reservation, payment and finalization, and how the system recovers without losing or duplicating a purchase.

The backend should be authoritative for prices, availability, payment status and key ownership. Don't expose license keys until an order has been successfully completed, and make sure users can only access their own orders.

Implement this in stages. Start with the backend services, state transitions and PostgreSQL integration tests, including concurrent requests and failure scenarios. Once those are verified, build the cart, checkout and order-history UI using the existing design system.

For the UI, make the flow feel like a proper digital marketplace. Include clear seller details, price breakdowns, stock changes, payment confirmation, and digital-key delivery. No real payments or third-party payment credentials.

Test the complete purchase journey in a real browser, including refresh, duplicate submissions, expired reservations, failed payments and signed-out users.

Run the full tests, production checks and secrets audit before deployment. Report the transaction design, failure handling, test results and any limitations.

## Review of the existing models

The foundations held (stock is the count of available keys, one payment per order, one order per idempotency key, key states enforced by a constraint). Four things needed correcting, done as a new migration:

- **No order deadline.** Only the keys carried `reserved_until`; orders gained `expires_at`.
- **Unsafe expiry.** The original plan let the next buyer take overdue keys directly. If the first buyer had been charged and only our bookkeeping crashed, their keys would have been sold twice.
- **States not explicit enough.** Orders and payments gained `cancelled`; an unpaid order's payment is no longer labelled "failed".
- **Idempotency ignored the payload.** Orders gained `cart_hash`, so a reused key with a different cart is refused.

## Transaction design

A purchase is three steps in two requests:

| Step | Request | Transaction |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Reserve | `POST /api/orders` | One: re-price the cart, create the pending order and payment, hold the keys for 10 minutes |
| 2. Charge | `POST /api/orders/{id}/pay` | None open while the gateway is asked |
| 3. Record | same request | One: lock the order row, then keys, payment and order change together |

- **Order states:** `pending` → `paid`, `failed`, `expired` or `cancelled`. None is ever left once reached.
- **Payment states:** `pending` → `succeeded`, `failed` or `cancelled`.
- **Key states:** `available` → `reserved` → `sold`, or back to `available`.
- **Separation:** `InventoryAllocator` knows keys and nothing about payments; `PaymentGateway` knows charging and nothing about orders; `SettleOrder` is the only code that moves an order out of `pending`.
- **The safety rule:** the gateway decides a reference (the order's public id) once. The first charge or cancel fixes the outcome and every later call returns it. Keys are sold only after a confirmed charge and released only after a confirmed decline or cancellation.
- **No card form:** the buyer picks one of three test cards (approved, declined, insufficient funds), so no card data is ever asked for.

## Failure handling

| Situation | Outcome |
|---|---|
| Two buyers want the last key | `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`: one gets it, the other is told at once (409 with a fresh quote) |
| Same order request repeated or sent several times at once | One order; every copy gets it |
| "Pay" pressed repeatedly | One charge, keys delivered once |
| Price or stock changed | Nothing reserved; the cart shows what changed and the buyer must accept it |
| Payment declined | Keys back on sale immediately; retrying is a new order |
| Buyer never pays | The order expires: cancelled at the gateway first, then keys released |
| Crash after reserving | The buyer can still pay until the deadline, otherwise it expires |
| Crash after charging, before recording | The next request that touches the order records it; the buyer gets the keys even if they never return |
| Crash while recording | The transaction rolls back; same as the row above |
| Pay and cancel at the same moment | Exactly one wins, and every request reports that result |

Access: every order route needs a signed-in user, and someone else's order answers "not found". Keys appear only in a paid order's own response.

The concurrency tests run real parallel processes, each with its own database connection: eight buyers for three keys, the same request six times, "Pay" six times, pay against cancel.

## Browser findings (local, full journey)

The journey worked end to end: guest cart, sign-in and return, refresh mid-checkout, declined payment, approved payment with keys, a reservation expiring on screen, and signed-out access. It also found three problems the automated tests had missed, all fixed with tests that fail without the fix:

- **Self-blocking:** after starting a checkout and returning to the cart, the buyer's own reservation made the offer look "no longer available". A buyer's quote now counts keys their own unpaid order holds.
- **Swallowed click:** a background refresh disabled "Proceed to checkout" at the moment of clicking when the tab regained focus.
- **Duplicate requests:** three presses in the same instant sent three pay requests. The server charged once, but the page now sends one.

While writing the live checks I also found and fixed a race where a duplicate request could cancel the order its twin had just created.

On the live site I checked the guest cart, the sign-in prompt and the order-history prompt in the browser.

## Limitations

- The cart is kept in the browser: it does not follow a buyer to another device.
- There is no background worker on the free tier. Overdue orders are settled by the next request that reads stock or orders; search suggestions and filter counts skip that step and can briefly show an expired hold as sold out.
- One checkout in progress per buyer, one payment attempt per order, at most 10 of an offer and 20 offers per order.
- No refunds, invoices, taxes, emails or seller payouts; a paid order cannot be cancelled.
- With a real payment provider, the expiry clean-up should move from page requests to a scheduled job.

These are recorded in `README.md` and `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`.

One commit with the latest conversation log is saved locally and not pushed. Each live verification run leaves a throwaway account in production, now with two unpaid test orders. I rebuilt and re-seeded the local development database after testing.

## Homepage outline

**API: `GET /api/homepage`** (public). One response; the frontend renders it top to bottom.

```json
{ "data": { "blocks": [
  { "id": 1, "type": "hero_banner",  "title": null,           "link": null,
    "data": { "banners": [ { "title", "subtitle", "image_url", "link_url", "cta_label" } ] } },
  { "id": 2, "type": "best_sellers", "title": "Best sellers", "link": { "label": "See all", "url": "/store" },
    "data": { "products": [ /* same product shape as the store list */ ] } },
  { "id": 3, "type": "deals",        "title": "Top deals",    "link": null, "data": { "products": [] } },
  { "id": 4, "type": "featured",     "title": "Featured",     "link": null, "data": { "products": [] } }
] } }
```

**Resolvers** (`app/Services/Homepage`, one Laravel app, no microservices). `homepage_blocks` rows decide which blocks exist, their order, title, link and settings; each type has its own class with its own data source:

| Block type | Resolver | Reads from |
|---|---|---|
| `hero_banner` | `HeroBannerResolver` | `banners` table |
| `best_sellers` | `BestSellersResolver` | products by sales count |
| `deals` | `DealsResolver` | products whose best offer is discounted, biggest discount first |
| `featured` | `FeaturedProductsResolver` | product slugs listed in the block's own config, in that order |

`BuildHomepage` loads the active rows in position order and asks the matching resolver for each block's data.

**Fallback behaviour**
- A block with nothing to show (no banners, no deals) is left out, never sent empty.
- A row with an unknown type is skipped.
- A resolver that throws loses only its own block; the error is logged and the rest of the page is returned.
- Product blocks show only products that can be bought now; a featured slug that no longer exists is skipped.
- Banner links must be paths inside the site; anything else is dropped.
- No active blocks, or the API failing: the frontend shows a plain introduction with a link to the store (and "Try again" on failure). A block type the frontend does not know is skipped.

**Acceptance criteria**
1. Reordering, hiding, retitling or resizing a block is a database row change, with no frontend change.
2. The frontend contains no product lists, banner texts or section titles.
3. Prices and stock on the homepage equal the store's for the same product.
4. One broken block never breaks the page.
5. The number of queries does not grow with the catalog.
6. No horizontal page scroll at 375 px; one `h1`, block titles as `h2`.

## Live purchase

One purchase on the live site with a throwaway test account and the simulated "approved" test card: 92 of 92 checks passed. No money or payment credentials were involved, and no key value appears in the output (keys are compared by SHA-256 fingerprint; I checked the whole output for key-shaped text and found none).

| What you asked | Result on the live site |
|---|---|
| Reserved key delivered correctly | Pay sent three times at once: one key delivered once, same key in all three answers |
| Survives a refresh | Order re-read twice: same key |
| Only through the buyer's order history | In the buyer's history as paid, and opening it from there shows the same key. Signed out: 401. A second signed-in account: "not found", zero orders. The history list, catalog and cart responses do not contain it |
| Order and payment consistent | Order paid with a payment time, payment succeeded, total equals the offer price and the sum of lines, one key per unit, nothing left pending. Paying or cancelling again changes nothing |
| Key cannot be sold again | The offer went from 4 keys to 3; all 3 remaining could be held and not one more |

The purchase ran through the API the way the browser does (same origin, session cookie, CSRF). I did not view the delivered key in a signed-in browser on the live site, because that would mean typing account credentials there; that page was verified in the browser locally.

## Recovery after a successful payment

- **Returning buyer:** opening the order already recorded an unrecorded charge. Opening the order history now does too.
- **Gateway unreachable:** the buyer still sees the order as it stands. The failure is logged, nothing is decided and the keys stay held.
- **Several tabs at once:** a charged order reconciled from six processes together is completed once.
- **Background worker:** `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` now has a table of what a real deployment would move to one: timely expiry, reconciliation shortly after an unrecorded payment, gateway calls in a queue with retries, provider webhooks, alerting on stuck orders, and key delivery by email.
- **Contract examples:** recorded example responses now mask their keys.

## Homepage

`GET /api/homepage` returns the active rows of `homepage_blocks` in position order. Each block type has its own resolver class with its own data source:

| Block | Resolver | Source |
|---|---|---|
| `hero_banner` | `HeroBannerResolver` | `banners` table |
| `best_sellers` | `BestSellersResolver` | products by sales count |
| `deals` | `DealsResolver` | best in-stock price is a discount, biggest first |
| `featured` | `FeaturedProductsResolver` | slugs listed in the row itself |

- **Frontend:** it maps a block type to a component and draws blocks in the order received. It holds no product, banner text or section title of its own.
- **Proof it is backend-driven:** in the local browser I moved, retitled and shrank the deals block and hid the featured block by editing rows only; the page followed with no frontend change.
- **Fallbacks:** an empty block is left out, an unknown type is skipped, a failing resolver loses only its own block, and links or artwork pointing outside the site are dropped. With no blocks or no API, the page shows a short introduction that leads to the store.
- **Layout:** a hero with one large banner and two tiles, a ranked best-sellers chart, a deals band with each discount on top, and a featured spotlight with four further picks.

## Remaining issues

- The homepage has no admin screen: changing it means editing `homepage_blocks` or `banners` rows. The seeder fills an empty layout once and never touches it again.

## Requirement checklist against the assignment PDF

| Requirement | Status | Gap |
|---|---|---|
| Store: browse, filter, sort, search by name | Met, tested, live | None |
| Homepage: blocks, each from its own backend service | Met, tested, live | No admin screen (documented) |
| Search from anywhere, typos and partial names | Met, tested, live | Abbreviations and accents not handled (documented) |
| Product page: details and offers from several sellers | Met, tested, live | None |
| Checkout: cart, pay, receive key | Met, tested, one live purchase verified | H1, M1 below |
| Authentication, protected checkout and order history | Met, tested, live | H1, M4 below |
| Eneba styling on every screen | Mostly met | M5–M7: header, footer and type scale are the weakest match |
| React + TypeScript, PHP backend, REST, database | Met | None |
| GitHub link, public URL on a free tier | Met | Live site runs `52a42aa`, which matches `origin/main` |
| Full AI chat history in an md file | Met | M10 below |
| "You must be able to explain your code" / justify scope | Weakened by the docs | H2 below |

## High priority

**H1. A signed-out browser still shows the previous buyer's orders.** After signing out, pressing Back on the order history shows "Sign in to see your orders" with the previous account's orders listed underneath. I reproduced this in the browser. The order page itself replaced the key with a sign-in prompt once the server answered; the code review says the cached key shows until then, and stays if that request fails. Fix: clear the cached orders on sign-out and on any "not signed in" answer, with a regression test.

**H2. The README contradicts itself.** A reviewer reading it will doubt what works. Fix: rewrite these parts.
- It still says "Nothing can be bought yet … no add to cart button" and "The landing page is a placeholder".
- `make prod-check` is described as checking sign-in only.
- It says the cold start "has not been observed yet".
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` still says authentication is "awaiting proof on the deployed site" and refers to "Planned" sections that no longer exist.
- `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` reports a live run against an old commit.
- There is no "how to try it" walkthrough (sign up, which test card does what) and no screenshot of the homepage, cart or checkout.

**H3. A request with an `Authorization: Bearer` header gets a 500.** I reproduced this locally and on the live site. Sanctum looks for a token table that was never created. No data is exposed, but any API client or scanner that sends this header sees a server error. Fix: one line telling Sanctum to ignore bearer tokens, plus a test.

## Medium priority

| # | Issue | Impact | Proposed fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| M1 | "One checkout per buyer" is not enforced under parallel requests (read in code; my local server handles one request at a time, so not reproduced) | One account could hold many keys for 10 minutes at a time, at no cost | Partial unique index on pending orders per user, retry once, parallel test |
| M2 | Crafted input returns 500 instead of 422: `min_price` above the integer range (reproduced live), invalid bytes in a filter (local), email sent as a list (read in code) | Looks unhandled; no leak | Tighten three validation rules, with tests |
| M3 | Seeded keys can be computed from the public seeder | Already in the README as a limitation; a real key would need a secret | Derive keys with the app key for new stock, or keep as documented. Your call |
| M4 | Account page is the odd one out: "The API is not reachable." with no retry, raw error text, no pending state | A reviewer who opens Account while the server wakes hits a dead end | Reuse the shared notice with "Try again" |
| M5 | Header and footer are the weakest match to Eneba: no category row, the header gives no sign of being signed in, one-line footer | First thing a reviewer compares | Category links to existing store filters, account name and Orders link when signed in, a fuller footer |
| M6 | Headings differ by phase: page titles are 30, 26 and 24 px; weight is 700 on most pages and 900 on the homepage; top spacing varies | Pages feel built at different times | Two shared title styles (Eneba uses 30 px / 700) and one top spacing |
| M7 | Buttons are re-declared per page: some lack disabled styling, the cursor differs between links and buttons | Inconsistent interaction states | Put the states in the two shared button styles and reuse them |
| M8 | No error boundary; favicon is the Vite starter logo; no page description | A render error would replace the whole app with a stack trace; the tab icon is not ours | One error route, own favicon, meta tags |
| M9 | Cart says "Sign in to check out" to a signed-in buyer while the session check is pending or failed | Confusing during cold start | Show the disabled checkout button while checking |
| M10 | The log's README does not say the session was compacted, and my macOS username appears once in a quoted path | Redaction disclosure is otherwise in place (header count and README section) | Add a sentence on compaction; decide whether to redact the username |
| M11 | Cold start: the first live API response took 25 s, during which pages show skeletons with no explanation | Likely the reviewer's first impression | "Server is waking up" hint after a few seconds; update the README with the measured time |

## Low priority (fix only if cheap)

- Two crossing multi-seller payments can deadlock and return 500 after the charge; it self-heals on the next visit.
- Catalog routes have no rate limit.
- Renaming a game in the data files would break the next deploy.
- No security headers on the frontend (frame, sniffing, referrer).
- The homepage shifts 16 px when it loads.
- Changing a filter scrolls to the top.
- Six full-page states have no `h1`.
- Opening a typed address in the same tab inherits the last scroll position.
- Focus is lost after payment.
- Deal cards show the discount twice.
- No automated check proves CSRF enforcement (it is enforced: 419 locally).

## What passed

- **Browser, three widths:** no sideways scroll at 375, 768 or 1440 px; no broken images; no text below 4.5:1 contrast where measurable.
- **Keyboard:** logical order, visible focus ring everywhere, search suggestions work by arrow keys.
- **States:** distinct empty, not-found and error states with retry on every page except Account; the only console errors were the expected 401 and 404.
- **Security review:** order access, key exposure, mass assignment, session handling, cookies, SQL injection and the checkout races all came back clean.
- **Suites:** 253 backend tests, 126 frontend tests, 110 of 110 local production checks, secrets audit clean.

## Not done

- Live verification was not re-run, since each run leaves a throwaway account in production; I'll run it with the production check and audit after the fixes, before any push.
- Two conversation-log commits are saved locally and not pushed.
- The local database now holds two test accounts and eight test orders from this audit.

## Proposed order

1. H1, H3, M1, M2 with regression tests.
2. M4–M9 and M11 as one consistency pass over shared styles, not page rewrites.
3. H2 and M10 documentation last, so it describes the final state.
4. Full local, production and live verification, audit, then push on your approval.

M3 and the low items are yours to include or leave.

## Fixes

| Item | What was wrong | What changed |
|---|---|---|
| H1 sign-out | Back after signing out showed the previous buyer's orders | Any sign-in, sign-up, sign-out or 401 now discards cached orders, keys and the checkout in progress. Order pages request and draw data only for a confirmed session, and the cache is stored per buyer |
| H3 bearer token | `Authorization: Bearer` caused a 500 | A bearer token is now treated as no token; session sign-in is unchanged |
| M1 one pending checkout | Six parallel checkouts by one buyer left six pending orders (reproduced in a test) | Partial unique index on pending orders per user. The losing request has held nothing; it cancels the order that beat it and retries once, then answers 409 `checkout_in_progress`. The migration first settles any existing duplicates |
| M2 malformed input | Oversized price filter, invalid UTF-8 in a filter, and an email sent as a list each gave a 500 | All now answer 422 |
| Cross-order deadlock | Two payments sharing sellers in opposite order deadlocked after the charge (reproduced in a test) | Sales counters are raised in a fixed order (products by id, then sellers by id). Recording a decision is also retried up to three times on a deadlock |
| M4–M9 consistency | Three title sizes, re-declared buttons, bare account page, wrong cart prompt, no error boundary, Vite favicon | One set of title, spacing and button styles used on every page; a category row and footer built from the product types the API returns; signed-in name and Orders link in the header; account page on shared states; crash page inside the shell; own favicon and page description |
| M11 cold start | Skeletons with no explanation for 25 s | After four seconds without an answer a notice says the server may be waking up. Failures are worded by cause: no answer or a 502/503/504 reads as "may be waking up", a JSON 500 reads as a server error |

Predictable demo keys are left as a documented limitation, as you asked.

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Backend tests | 274 passed (was 253) |

| Live site in the browser, signed out, 1440 and 375 px | no console errors, no broken images, no sideways scroll |

## 1. Single-pending checkout

**What happened.** Two checkouts by one buyer with different idempotency keys, call them A and B:

1. Both look for the buyer's other pending orders and find none.
2. Both insert an order row. A's insert succeeds; B's waits at the unique index for A's transaction.
3. A holds its keys, commits, and its caller is answered 201.
4. B's insert is refused by the index. B has held nothing, because the order row is the first thing saved.
5. The flaw: B then cancelled A's order and created its own.

Step 5 was wrong. A's order had already been returned as created, and nobody had chosen to replace it. Copies of one request (same key) were never affected; this only hit different keys.

**The fix** (in `PlaceOrder`, no architecture change):
- The losing request cancels nothing.
- If it was for the same cart, it is answered with the winner's order.
- If it was for a different cart, it is refused with 409 `checkout_in_progress`.
- Checking out the same cart again under another key (another tab or device) now returns the checkout in progress instead of replacing it. I extended the rule to this sequential case so the outcome does not depend on arriving a millisecond before or after the winner's commit.
- A deliberately changed cart still supersedes the earlier order, as before.

**Tests.** Two new tests fix the interleaving across two processes and connections rather than relying on timing. The first request pauses after its insert with the transaction open, the second blocks on the index, and the first commits only when `pg_locks` shows the wait. Both failed on the old code (two orders, the winner cancelled) and pass now, three runs in a row.

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Backend tests | 278 passed |
| Frontend tests | 149 passed |
| Local production check | 119 of 119, including a new same-cart check |
| Secrets audit | passed |

**Is a rewrite necessary? No.** Nothing in the history is a credential. The account name is low-sensitivity and close to your public GitHub handle. A rewrite would change every commit hash and need a force-push, and GitHub can keep old commits reachable by hash afterwards. I'd leave it; it's your call.

## Overlapping checkouts on the live site

I added two checks to the verification script for this. All four below ran live with a throwaway account.

| Sent | Answered | Afterwards |
|---|---|---|
| Same request three times at once | 201, 200, 200 | one order |
| Same cart from two more tabs while that order is unpaid | 200, 200 | same order, still unpaid |
| Same cart from three tabs at once, nothing in progress | 201, 200, 200 | one unpaid order |
| Two different carts at once | 201 and 409 `checkout_in_progress` | one unpaid order: the accepted one |

In the last row the refused request cancelled nothing. If the two requests do not overlap, the later one legitimately replaces the earlier as a changed cart; the check accepts that too, and fails on two unpaid orders or on an accepted order being cancelled by a refused one. The two-connection tests force the overlap, so they remain the deterministic proof.

## Screenshots

Open [design-review/index.html](design-review/index.html) for every page side by side, before and after, at 1440, 768 and 375 px. The folder also holds the eneba.com reference captures and the deployed site as captured today. It is excluded from Git on this machine, because the reference pictures are Eneba's.

## Biggest differences from eneba.com, in priority order

1. **Header:** ours was text links and a large yellow "Search" block; Eneba uses icons, a single bordered search field and a quiet category bar.
2. **Type and surfaces:** nearly every label was set at the heaviest weight, and cards had no edge, so pages looked flat and loud.
3. **Product cards:** the platform floated as a badge over the artwork, and prices did not line up across a row.
4. **Product page:** on a phone the price and "Add to cart" sat below the cover, facts and description; the offers table put the price far from its button.
5. **Homepage:** blocks used different card sizes and link styles, with no rhythm between sections.
6. **Not done:** Eneba's typeface, mega-menu, category icon row, payment logos and product screenshots.

## The five improvements

- **Shared tokens and surfaces:**
  - A band colour, and the two heaviest weights one step lighter.
  - Avenir Next ahead of the system font on Apple devices.
  - One transition speed for colour changes.
  - Two shared surfaces with a thin edge, now used by every card, row, form and panel.
  - A pressed state and a spinner on buttons.
- **Header:** my own line-drawn icons for cart, orders and account, a count badge, one bordered search field, and an underline on the current category. On a phone the links are icons only.
- **Product card:** a thin edge that turns teal on hover, the platform as a strip under the artwork, and two title lines always reserved so prices align. The store, search results and homepage all use it.
- **Product page:**
  - On a phone, a small cover sits beside the title, followed directly by the price and "Add to cart".
  - The buy box stays in view on a desktop.
  - Each offer is one row with the seller's initial, a star rating and the price beside the button; only the best price gets the yellow button.
- **Homepage:** alternating full-width bands, "see more" as a button, and one six-column card row for deals and picks.

Cart and checkout pick up the shared surfaces. I also moved the cart price under the product name on a phone, and made the order summaries stay in view on a desktop.

## Results

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Frontend tests, lint, type check | 149 passed; clean |
| Backend tests | 278 passed |
| Production build | builds; about 1 KB larger gzipped |
| Local production check, including a purchase | 121 of 121 |
| Secrets audit | passed |
| Pages at 375, 768 and 1440 px | 13 pages, no sideways overflow, no broken images, no console errors |
| Accessibility, before against after | no new notes on any of 41 page views |

The accessibility check is my own script, not a standard audit tool. It covers contrast, control names, field labels, heading order, keyboard order and visible focus. The only notes are the same small touch targets that exist today (a few text links and the payment radio buttons on phones).

One test failed during the work because the featured product had become a list item; I restored the markup rather than change the test.

## What to know before adopting it

- **Other platforms are untested.** Windows and Android will use their own system font with the lighter weights; I expect that to look fine but have not seen it.
- **Deliberate behaviour changes:** phone header links are icons only (names kept for screen readers), and offers other than the best price have an outlined button.
- **Documentation is untouched.** The architecture notes do not yet mention the new shared pieces (`surface`, `raised`, `Icon`).
- **The conversation log was not regenerated**, as you asked; `make ai-log` is still owed for this session.
- **Key fragments were printed.** A tool output printed the first 13 characters of four demo keys from the local database. I extended the unpublished export's redaction rule to cover cut-short keys.

Here is the proposal. Nothing was built, generated, merged or deployed, and no credits were spent. I recommend an image sequence drawn to a canvas, attached to the existing "Top deals" block, with Higgsfield limited to stills and look reference.

## Where it fits

The homepage on `design-refresh` draws whatever blocks the API returns, in the API's order, one component per block type. The cinematic section should not be a new block or a fixed slot. It should be a presentation of the existing `deals` block:

- The backend still decides whether that block exists, where it sits, its title and its six products.
- The frontend wraps that block in a pinned stage. The handoff state is the block's normal markup: the same six `ProductCard`s it renders today.
- If the animation cannot run, the block renders exactly as it does now. No API change.

I chose `deals` because it is already one row of six cards on a darker band, which is the natural landing shape. The hero stays first and untouched.

## Storyboard

| Scroll | Beat | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| 0–10% | Opening | Dark hall in the brand purple. Camera low and close on the edge of one box, rim-lit. |
| 10–55% | Travel | Camera glides down a corridor of shelves; boxes pass on both sides with parallax; accent light strips in teal and yellow. |
| 55–80% | Turn | The corridor opens. Six boxes leave the shelves and line up facing the camera while it rises, pulls back and the lens lengthens. |
| 80–95% | Lock | The six boxes settle flat in a row. Scene lighting fades to the exact band colour of the page. |
| 95–100% | Handoff | The real cards' covers fade in over the boxes at the same rectangles; names, prices, discounts and buttons appear beneath. The stage unpins. |

Scrolling back plays the same frames in reverse. On phones the framing is portrait, the corridor is a single wall, and the boxes land two across on the first two cards.

## Canvas image sequence or seekable video

| | Image sequence on canvas | Seekable video |
|---|---|---|
| Frame accuracy | Exact, same in reverse | Depends on keyframes; Safari seeks late and snaps |
| Safari and iOS | Works | Slow seeks; iOS may not preload without a tap |
| Size | Larger | Smaller, unless every frame is a keyframe |
| Control over loading | Full (coarse pass first, then fill in) | Little |
| Memory | Must be managed | Handled by the browser |

I recommend the image sequence. Frame-exact alignment at the handoff is the hard requirement, and video scrubbing is least reliable in Safari.

## Desktop and mobile

| | Desktop (1024 px and up) | Phone (under 640 px) |
|---|---|---|
| Render | 1440×810, about 120 frames | 720×1280, about 72 frames |
| Target size | 6 MB total, first pass under 800 KB | 2.5 MB total, first pass under 350 KB |
| Pinned scroll length | about 2.5 screens | about 2 screens |
| Scroll per frame | about 17 px | about 19 px |
| Decoded frames kept | about 30 | about 16 (iOS memory limits) |

Those sizes are my estimates for WebP frames of a dark scene; the prototype will measure them.

- **Loading:** the block renders normally first. Frames load only when the browser is idle, every eighth frame first so scrubbing works early. The pinned mode switches on only if the section is still below the screen, so the page never jumps under the visitor.
- **Responsiveness:** native scrolling only, no scroll hijacking. Scroll position is read once per display frame and eased over about 100 ms so wheel steps do not look stepped.
- **Safari:** stage height uses small-viewport units so the address bar does not cause jumps. The `overflow-x: clip` already on the shell does not break pinning.
- **Fallbacks:** with reduced motion, Save-Data, a slow connection or failed frames, there is no pin, no canvas and no extra scroll, just the ordinary block.
- **Tablets (640–1023 px):** the block is three across there, which would need a third render. I propose the ordinary block for version one.
- **Keyboard:** focusing a card jumps the stage to the handoff, so focus is never on something invisible. The canvas is hidden from screen readers.

## Blender scene plan

- **Boxes:** 3:4 fronts matching the card covers, textured with our own generated covers (34 exist). No official artwork or logos.
- **Camera:** one camera on a path. The focal length increases toward the end so the final row is nearly flat and the box fronts are true rectangles.
- **Lighting and colour:** emissive strips in the brand tokens. The view transform is Standard, so the last frames end on exactly `#341281` and the canvas edge is invisible.
- **No motion blur:** a visitor can stop on any frame, and blurred frames look smeared when still.
- **Alignment data:** a script exports the screen rectangle of each of the six box fronts, plus the product slugs they were textured with.
- **Reproducible:** the scene is built by a Python script run with Blender headless, so the render can be repeated.

## Matching baked boxes to live products

The six landing boxes are rendered with the covers of the products in the deals block at render time, recorded in a manifest. At runtime:

- If the live block has the same six products in the same order, the handoff is seamless.
- If not, the boxes dissolve as the real cards fade in.

Either way a baked image is never presented as a product. The catch is that the frames go stale when the homepage content changes, until they are re-rendered.

## Asset pipeline

1. Convert our cover SVGs to PNG textures.
2. Fetch the deals block from the API and write the slot manifest.
3. Build and render the scene in Blender, once per layout.
4. Encode frames as WebP, which Blender can write itself.
5. Export the handoff rectangles and manifest.
6. Check the size budget and store the frames under a versioned folder with long-lived caching.

## Higgsfield

Regenerating frames with a video model changes geometry slightly from frame to frame, which breaks both the final alignment and the reverse scroll. So I would limit it to:

- **Still background plates or light textures** that Blender then renders through.
- **One enhanced style frame** as a lighting reference, reproduced by hand in Blender.
- **Optionally the opening beat only**, accepted only if box edges stay within a pixel or two of the Blender render and there is no flicker. It would never be used for the last quarter of the sequence.

## Prototype strategy

1. **Scroll harness with placeholder frames (no Blender):** on a new branch from `design-refresh`. This proves pinning, easing, loading, memory, the fallbacks and Safari behaviour.
2. **Grey-box Blender blockout at low resolution:** proves the camera move and the handoff alignment at 1440, 1024 and 375 px.
3. **Look development:** materials, lighting and, if you want it, one Higgsfield style frame.
4. **Final renders:** budgets and tests, then your review before any merge.

Each step ends with something you can look at, and the existing homepage tests keep passing because the block falls back to its normal form.

## What I checked on this machine

- Blender is installed, but the plugin connection is currently off. The headless route above does not need the plugin.
- `ffmpeg` is installed.

## Decisions for you

- **Placement:** attach it to the deals block, as proposed, or to a different block.
- **Tablets:** accept the ordinary block in version one, or add a third render.
- **Repository size:** the frames add roughly 8 MB of binary files. They can live in Git or be kept out and built at deploy time.

I have no record of the Neighbourhood Pizza project in this session or in my saved notes, so I followed the workflow as you described it: Blender for geometry and camera, its renders as motion and appearance references.

## Blender blockout and planned motion

The scene is built entirely by a script and is a function of scroll progress, so every frame is repeatable in both directions. As a clip the desktop move is 5 seconds at 24 frames a second (120 frames); the phone version is 4 seconds.

| Time (of 5 s) | Camera | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| 0–0.6 s | Low, near the left wall, turned 15° inward, 30 mm | Opening on the first cases |
| 0.6–2.75 s | Glides to the centre line, 28 mm | Travel down the corridor of illuminated cases |
| 2.75–3.5 s | Rises slightly, lens to 38 mm | Through the yellow portal into the chamber |
| 2.9–4.3 s | Settles square-on, 50 mm | Six cases glide into one flat row; they turn at most 27°, never spin |
| 4.3–5 s | Still | Hold, while the page fades the real cards in |

The chamber is still an empty void in the blockout; it needs architecture that fades to the page colour.

## Reference assets

All in `design-review/cinematic/reference/` (local, not in Git):

- **Motion reference:** `motion-wide.mp4` (1280×720, 5 s) and `motion-narrow.mp4` (720×1280, 4 s), each with its exact first and last frame as PNG.
- **Plain variants:** the same moves with blank glowing panels instead of covers. I recommend these as the model input, because a video model will turn cover lettering into nonsense or invent artwork.
- **Stills:** five per layout at 1920×1080 and 1080×1920, at the storyboard beats.

## Higgsfield options

| Option | How it uses the Blender move | Limits | Cost for about 5 s |
|---|---|---|---|
| Kling 4.0, Edit video | Restyles the clip itself | 3–30 s input, 720p or 1080p | 13.75 credits at 720p, 20 at 1080p (the same page's FAQ says 25 and 37.5) |
| Seedance 2.5, motion reference | Replicates camera behaviour in a newly generated scene | 4–30 s, up to 1080p | about 35 at 720p, about 15 at 480p (halved from the published 10-second price) |
| Kling 4.0, keyframes | Up to 10 Blender stills as fixed moments; motion between is the model's | 3–30 s | as Kling above |

Reference inputs can change the cost, and the exact figure only appears on the Generate button or in the plugin's estimate.

**Recommendation: Kling 4.0 Edit video.** It is the only option that starts from our actual frames, so the camera path and composition are inherited rather than imitated. Seedance 2.5 is the backup.

**Proposed test:** the desktop move at 720p through all three options, about 65 to 85 credits (roughly $3 to $4 on the Plus plan). I would judge each by overlaying it on the Blender frames and measuring how far case edges drift.

Whichever model wins, I would keep the formation and hold frames from Blender, put the real covers back using Blender's geometry, and keep the full Blender animation as the fallback.

## Scroll harness results (blockout frames)

| Window | Handoff | Cases off by | Needed before it starts | All frames |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1024×768 | dissolve | 4.1 px | 112 KB | 856 KB |
| 1280×800 | seamless | 0.01 px | 112 KB | 856 KB |
| 1440×900 | seamless | 0.01 px | 112 KB | 856 KB |
| 375×812 | seamless | 0 px | 50 KB | 371 KB |
| 768×1024 | ordinary block, as intended | – | 0 | 0 |

- **Scrolling:** 16.7 ms between frames, none over 33 ms, down and back up. This is headless Chrome on this Mac, not a phone, and Safari is untested.
- **Memory:** 26 MB of decoded pictures on desktop and 14 MB on the phone at blockout size. At final size the same cache would be about 140 MB and 59 MB, which is too much; it needs a smaller cache or resolution.
- **Real file sizes at final resolution:** 21.6 KB per desktop frame and 12.4 KB per phone frame (WebP, quality 75), so about 2.6 MB for 120 desktop frames. This is flat blockout content; cinematic footage will be heavier.
- **Pixel check:** edges measured from the drawn pixels read 1.5 to 4.7 px off, limited by the blur of low-resolution frames.

Two things went wrong and were fixed on the branch:

- **Pinning:** the stage scrolled away instead of staying pinned.
- **Arming:** the effect did not switch on at 1280 and 1440 px, because the deals block is already at the foot of the first screen there. I loosened the rule, which means the bottom edge of the first screen shifts once when it arms. That needs your decision.

The 149 existing tests still pass, plus 9 new ones for the scroll arithmetic.

> Use the reference video as the exact camera path, speed and layout for this shot: one continuous, slow, steady forward glide down the centre of a long corridor, ending through a glowing rectangular doorway into a dark open chamber, where six upright panels settle into one level row facing the camera and hold still for the last second. Keep every wall, pillar, shelf and panel in the same position, size and order as in the reference video. No cuts, no speed changes, no camera shake, no added camera moves.
>
> Re-light and re-surface the scene so it looks like a real, expensive private vault for a collection of digital games. Architecture: tall walls of dark stone and brushed metal with deep recessed bays between square pillars, a polished black stone floor with soft, slightly blurred reflections, and thin recessed light strips where the walls meet the ceiling and the floor. In each bay one slim upright display case of dark metal and glass stands on a floating shelf; its front is a softly glowing frosted-glass panel in a single flat colour, as in the reference images.
>
> Lighting: restrained deep violet ambient light, cool teal light spilling from under some of the shelves, and one thin warm yellow frame of light around the doorway at the end. A faint haze in the air, so the light strips bloom gently and the far end of the corridor falls into darkness. Strong sense of depth, shallow depth of field on the nearest panels, realistic bounce light and contact shadows. Cinematic, calm, premium.
>
> The glowing panels stay completely blank: no text, no letters, no numbers, no logos, no symbols, no pictures, no characters, no cover artwork, no user interface. No people. Nothing spins, drifts off or bursts; no particles, sparks or lens flares.
>
> The chamber at the end is empty and dark violet. The six panels finish evenly spaced in one straight row, upright and square to the camera.

## Technical fixes, measured

Frames are now the blockout at final size (1440×810 × 120 on desktop, 720×1280 × 96 on the phone), so these are real figures.

**Layout shift:** the section's space is reserved when the block first appears, from the window and device alone. With frames delayed to arrive 3.5 to 4.5 s after the page was drawn, page length and block positions were identical before and after at 1024, 1280, 1440 and 375 px, and the browser recorded zero layout shift.

**Memory:**

| | Desktop (1440) | Phone (375) |
|---|---|---|
| Budget for decoded frames | 48 MB | 24 MB |
| Most frames held at once | 10 (4.45 MB each) | 6 (3.52 MB each) |
| Page process: at rest, then peak while scrubbing | 481 → 532 MB | 387 → 418 MB |
| Graphics process: at rest, then peak | 110 → 131 MB | 83 → 97 MB |
| Frames held after leaving the section | not re-measured after the last fix | 0 |

Process memory comes from the operating system and is noisy; the rise while scrubbing is the meaningful figure (about 50 MB on desktop, 30 MB on the phone).

**Scrolling:** 16.7 ms between frames with none over 33 ms, at reading speed and in a fast flick, down and back up. This is headless Chrome on this Mac; real phones and Safari are untested.

**Loading:** 349 KB before the effect can start on desktop (160 KB on the phone), then 2.66 MB (1.21 MB) for all frames.

**Handoff:** seamless at 1280 and 1440 px and on the phone (0.01 px or less computed); dissolve at 1024 px, where the cases are 4.1 px off. A zoomed mid-crossfade screenshot at 1440 px shows no doubling.

**Fallbacks:** reduced motion, data saving, a 2g connection, a 2 GB device, a browser without picture decoding and tablets all reserve no space, download no frames and give the same page as before. If frames cannot be fetched, the block is shown and the pinned stretch is dropped.

All 158 frontend tests pass. The work is committed on the local branch `cinematic-prototype`.

- **A key was printed:** that inspection printed the add-on's settings, which include Higgsfield's own analytics key (shipped inside the add-on, not your credential). I added a rule to the unpublished transcript export to redact values like it.

The test ran once for exactly 35 credits, and my recommendation is to keep the Blender animation: the Seedance clip is clean but does not follow Blender's camera, and it added no realistic materials.

## What was run

- **Settings:** Seedance 2.5, References mode, 5 s, 720p, 16:9, standard bitrate, audio off, one video and three image references, no start or end frame.
- **Prompt:** sent unchanged; the model has no prompt-rewriting switch.
- **Cost:** 35 credits estimated on the uploaded files, 35 charged (balance 916 to 881).
- **Output:** one result, 1280×720, 24 frames a second, 5.04 s, no audio. Nothing was retried, upscaled or extended.

## Comparison with the Blender reference

Camera position is read from how wide the doorway appears: the closer the camera, the wider it is.

| Moment | Blender | Seedance |
|---|---|---|
| 0.0 s | under 5% of the frame | 14.4% |
| 1.0 s | 14.7% | 15.6% |
| 1.5 s | 18.1% | 17.8% |
| 2.5 s | 46.3% | 31.2% |
| Doorway top leaves the frame | about 2.6 s | about 3.1 s |

- **Camera path:** the clip starts where Blender is after one second, then barely moves for a second, so the first fifth of the corridor is never travelled. It matches Blender only from about 1.1 to 1.6 s.
- **Timing:** Blender accelerates into the doorway; Seedance keeps an even pace and goes through about 0.4 s late.
- **Six final panels:** all six arrive, in the right order and colours, level and at almost the right size. The row sits 9 to 12 px left and 10 px up, and wanders about 10 px before settling at 4.6 s. The worst edge is 14 px from Blender's card positions; the handoff needs 1 px.
- **Geometry:** walls, pillars, shelves and light strips hold their places. The coloured cases in the bays turned into grey-violet slabs while the floor still reflects the old colours.
- **Lighting and materials:** violet, teal and yellow are kept, and the doorway glow is a real improvement. There is no stone, metal or glass; it is the blockout with bloom. The stills were polished blockout and the model copied them, not the prompt.
- **Flicker:** none; it is steadier than the reference.
- **Doorway transition:** smooth, but the outer two panels are cut by the door frame and one is half transparent for a few frames. The chamber beyond is pure black, not the page's violet that the cards dissolve into.
- **Forbidden content:** no text, logos, artwork, people or particles.

## Recommendation

Retain the original Blender animation. Blending is not worth it: the two clips agree for only half a second mid-corridor, and re-timing cannot restore the missing start.

This is one run, so it shows this reference package fails, not that Seedance cannot follow a camera. A second attempt would need different inputs: appearance stills with real materials, the first frame pinned to Blender's, and a corridor-only section. That would be a new spend for you to approve.

## Things to know

- **How it was submitted:** through the Higgsfield add-on's own client, since the connector has no video tool. The four reference files and the job are now in your Higgsfield account.
- **Add-on install is incomplete:** its built-in parameter check cannot run because `jsonschema` is not installed in this Blender. I ran the same check outside Blender from the add-on's bundled copy and it passed; I did not change the installation. Reinstalling the add-on should fix it.
- **Git:** the comparison tool is committed on the local `cinematic-prototype` branch (`5869a0e`). The checkout is back on `main`.
- **Not touched:** the Blender handoff frames and the website.
- **Measurement limits:** camera position is inferred from the doorway's size, not recovered in 3D, and panel positions are accurate to about 3 px.

- REPORT.md
